r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
We have a directed graph with N vertices, numbered 1, 2, \ldots, N.
Information about the edges is given by N^2 characters C_{1, 1}, C_{1, 2}, \ldots, C_{1, N}, C_{2, 1}, \ldots, C_{N, N}. Here, each C_{i, j} is either a lowercase English letter or -.
If C_{i, j} is a lowercase English letter, then there is exactly one directed edge from vertex i to vertex j labeled C_{i, j}. If C_{i, j} is -, there is no edge from vertex i to vertex j.
For each integer pair (i, j) with 1 \leq i, j \leq N, answer the following question:

- Among all (not necessarily simple) paths from vertex i to vertex j whose concatenation of labels on the edges forms a palindrome, what is the length of the shortest such path? If there is no such path, the answer is -1.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
C_{1, 1}C_{1, 2}\ldotsC_{1, N}
C_{2, 1}C_{2, 2}\ldotsC_{2, N}
\vdots
C_{N, 1}C_{N, 2}\ldotsC_{N, N}

Output

Let A_{i, j} be the answer to the question for the pair (i, j). Print them in the following format:
A_{1, 1} A_{1, 2} \ldots A_{1, N}
A_{2, 1} A_{2, 2} \ldots A_{2, N}
\vdots
A_{N, 1} A_{N, 2} \ldots A_{N, N}

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 100
- N is an integer.
- Each C_{i, j} is either a lowercase English letter or -.

Sample Input 1

4
ab--
--b-
---a
c---

Sample Output 1

0 1 2 4
-1 0 1 -1
3 -1 0 1
1 -1 -1 0

For example, consider the case (i, j) = (1, 4).
By taking the path 1 \to 1 \to 2 \to 3 \to 4, and concatenating the labels on its edges in order, we get the string abba, which is a palindrome.
There is no path of length at most 3 from vertex 1 to vertex 4 whose concatenation of labels is a palindrome. Thus, the answer for (1, 4) is 4.
Note that the empty string is also a palindrome.

Sample Input 2

5
us---
-st--
--s--
u--s-
---ts

Sample Output 2

0 1 3 -1 -1
-1 0 1 -1 -1
-1 -1 0 -1 -1
1 3 -1 0 -1
-1 -1 5 1 0

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Forward transitions from P(l,r):

Need prove that reverse BFS from terminals gives distances to P states in expanded graph. We can implement BFS from terminals along reverse edges. Let's define dist for all states. Initially dist[P(z,z)] = 0, dist[P(i,j)] = 1 if edge. Then:
For state P(l,r) with distance d, reverse edge to M(l, r_next, ch) for each outgoing edge r -> r_next label ch. Wait earlier from P(l',r') to M(l', r, ch) for edge r' -> r label ch. Let's rename to avoid confusion:
Let current P(a,b) (middle state). We know distance d = shortest palindrome path from a to b? Actually after reverse BFS, dist[P(a,b)] is answer for (a,b). To propagate to larger palindrome P(x,y) such that P(x,y) -> M(a? no) -> P(a,b):
Forward: P(x,y) -> M(u, y, ch) if edge x->u label ch. Then M(u,y,ch) -> P(u,v) if edge v->y label ch. So the resulting middle P(u,v). Thus reverse from P(u,v) to P(x,y) goes:
P(u,v) -> M(u, y, ch) if edge v->y label ch. Then M(u,y,ch) -> P(x,y) if edge x->u label ch.
So if current P(u,v), we need iterate outgoing edges from v: v -> y label ch, target M(u, y, ch). Then from M(u,y,ch), iterate incoming edges to u: x -> u label ch, target P(x,y). Good.

Thus in code:

    relax m_idx with d+1
When popping m_idx = mid_index(u, y, ch) (where u is left vertex, y is right vertex after consuming right edge? Actually M(u,y,ch) means we have consumed left edge into u and need right edge into y? Let's define M(l, r, ch) from forward: P(l_old, r_old) -> M(l_new, r_old, ch) after consuming left edge l_old->l_new. Then M(l_new, r_old, ch) -> P(l_new, r_new) if edge r_new -> r_old label ch. So M(u, y, ch) has left vertex u, right vertex y (the original right before consuming right edge). Reverse from P(u,v) to M(u, y, ch) if edge v->y label ch. Then from M(u,y,ch) to P(x,y) if edge x->u label ch. So yes, M(u,y,ch) stores left u, right y, label ch. The right edge to be consumed is from some v to y with label ch; after consuming, new right is v? Wait forward: M(u, y, ch) -> P(u, v) if edge v -> y label ch. So the new right vertex is v (predecessor of y). In reverse from P(u,v), we know v, and choose y successor of v with label ch, target M(u,y,ch). Then from M(u,y,ch), choose x predecessor of u with label ch, target P(x,y). The final larger palindrome P(x,y) has right vertex y. Good.

So code:
P(a,b) current.
For each edge b -> y with label ch:
    m = mid(a, y, ch)
    if dist[m] > d+1: set, push.
M(a, y, ch) current.
For each edge x -> a with label ch (incoming to a):
    p = x*N + y
    if dist[p] > d+1: set, push.

Need implement mid_index:
mid_base = N*N
mid_index(u, y, ch) = mid_base + (u * N + y) * 26 + ch
Because M states count N*N*26. P index = u*N+v.

When popping P state, we need decode u,v:
p_idx = dist? We can store queue of ints. To decode:
u = p_idx // N

For M state:
offset = p_idx - mid_base
pair = offset // 26

u = pair // N

Precompute:
N = int(input())
C = [input().strip() for _ in range(N)]
label_id = {chr(ord('a')+i): i for i in range(26)}
out_edges = [[] for _ in range(N)]  # for each vertex v, list of (to, ch)
incoming = [[[] for _ in range(26)] for _ in range(N)]  # incoming[v][ch] = list of from vertices
edges = [] # maybe for init
for i in range(N):
    for j,ch in enumerate(C[i]):
        if ch != '-':
            cid = ord(ch)-97
            out_edges[i].append((j,cid))
            incoming[j][cid].append(i)
            edges.append((i,j))
Note: If C[i][j] is '-', no edge. There is exactly one edge per pair if letter.

Initialization:
total = N*N*27
dist = [INF]*total
from collections import deque
q = deque()
# diagonals
for i in range(N):
    idx = i*N+i
    if dist[idx] > 0:
        dist[idx]=0; q.append(idx)
# direct edges
for i,j in edges:
    idx = i*N+j
    if dist[idx] > 1:
        dist[idx]=1; q.append(idx)
But if we append all diagonals first, then all edges, queue order okay. However, if an edge is diagonal self-loop, dist=0, skip. If an edge from i to j with i!=j, dist=1. If there are edges and diagonals, q contains 0s then 1s. Good.

But wait: What if a diagonal P(i,i) is also reachable as direct edge? We skip edge because dist=0. Good.

BFS:
while q:
    idx = q.popleft()
    d = dist[idx]
    if idx < N*N:
        u = idx // N
        v = idx - u*N  # or idx % N
        nd = d + 1
        # For each outgoing edge from v: v -> y label ch
        for y, ch in out_edges[v]:
            mid = mid_base + (u * N + y) * 26 + ch
            if dist[mid] > nd:
                dist[mid] = nd
                q.append(mid)
    else:
        offset = idx - mid_base
        pair = offset // 26
        ch = offset - pair*26  # or offset % 26
        u = pair // N
        y = pair - u*N
        nd = d + 1
        # For each incoming edge to u with label ch: x -> u
        for x in incoming[u][ch]:
            p = x * N + y
            if dist[p] > nd:
                dist[p] = nd
                q.append(p)

Now, let's consider if BFS expanded graph could compute a distance that doesn't correspond to a valid path due to intermediate state mismatch. We defined reverse transitions carefully. Let's prove by induction:
Let dist[P(a,b)] after BFS = L. Then there exists a palindrome path from a to b of length L.
Base: L=0 diagonal empty; L=1 direct edge.
Induction:
If dist[P(a,b)] = L+1? Actually BFS sets P(x,y) from M(u,y,ch) with d+1 where dist[M]=d. dist[M] set from P(u,v) with d-1? Let's trace:
Suppose dist[P(u,v)] = d (palindrome path u->v length d). Then for edge v->y label ch, M(u,y,ch) gets d+1. Then for edge x->u label ch, P(x,y) gets d+2. We need show palindrome path x->y length d+2: take edge x->u label ch, then palindrome path u->v length d, then edge v->y label ch. The label string is ch + palindrome + ch, which is palindrome. Yes. If d=1 direct edge, length3 works. If d=0 empty, length2 works. Good.
Conversely, any palindrome path length L>=2 can be decomposed into first edge x->u label ch, middle u->v palindrome length L-2, last edge v->y label ch. Then reverse BFS will reach P(x,y) via P(u,v) -> M(u,y,ch) -> P(x,y). So distances match shortest.

Let's verify total transitions more carefully:

Let's examine.

We initialize P(i,i)=0 (empty palindrome). We also have direct edge i->i label c, which is a palindrome of length1. For building a longer palindrome, the middle can be any palindrome path, including length1 self-loop. But to build odd-length palindromes with middle self-loop, we need source P(i,i) with dist1. However, dist[P(i,i)] is 0, which is smaller. Does using dist0 instead of dist1 ever fail to find an odd palindrome that requires the middle to be exactly the self-loop, not empty? Let's think.

Suppose we have a palindrome path from x to y: x --a--> i --c--> i --a--> y? Labels a c a, length3, middle is self-loop i->i label c. If we use dist[P(i,i)]=0 (empty), we can build length2: x--a->i, i--a->y, labels a a, not a c a. We cannot build length3 because the middle empty doesn't include the self-loop edge. But BFS also has direct edge source P(i,i)=1 if we initialize it. If we skip it because dist0, we may miss length3 palindrome. Is that possible? Wait, if there is a self-loop i->i label c, then P(i,i) has both empty path length0 and direct edge length1. The shortest palindrome from i to i is 0. But for building a larger palindrome, we need to add edges around a palindrome path from i to i. If we use the empty path, we get even length. If we use the self-loop path, we get odd length. The recurrence F[x][y] = min(2+F[u][v]) uses F[u][v] which is the shortest palindrome path from u to v, not all possible palindromes. For building the shortest palindrome from x to y, do we ever need to use a non-shortest palindrome as the middle? This is a crucial point.

The recurrence F[x][y] = min over first/last edges of 2 + F[u][v]. It uses the shortest palindrome path from u to v. If F[u][v] is 0 (empty) but there is also a length1 self-loop, using the length1 self-loop as middle gives length 2 + 1 = 3. Using empty gives length 2. For the same outer edges, the empty middle yields a shorter palindrome (length2) if the outer edges connect appropriately. But for an odd palindrome, the outer edges are different: first edge x->i label a, last edge i->y label a? Wait if middle is self-loop i->i, then first edge x->i, last edge i->y. For empty middle at i, first edge x->i, last edge i->y too! Actually if middle is empty at i, the path is x -> i -> y with labels a a. If middle is self-loop i->i, the path is x -> i -> i -> y with labels a c a. Both have the same first and last edges (x->i and i->y). The empty middle path is length2 and is a valid palindrome from x to y if edge x->i label a and edge i->y label a. The self-loop middle path is length3 and also valid. Since we want shortest, length2 is shorter, so the odd length3 is not needed for that pair (x,y). But what if the outer edges are not both present? To build odd palindrome with middle self-loop, we need first edge x->i and last edge i->y. If those exist, then the even palindrome with empty middle also exists (same edges) and is shorter. Thus using the shortest middle (empty) would give an even shorter palindrome for the same outer edges. Therefore, for minimizing overall length, using a non-shortest middle is never beneficial? Wait, the middle vertices u and v are determined by the outer edges. If we use a longer middle path from u to v, the outer edges are the same. Replacing the middle with a shorter palindrome from u to v yields a shorter path with the same outer edges and still palindrome. Thus the shortest palindrome from x to y will always use the shortest palindrome from u to v as middle. So F[u][v] (shortest) is sufficient. This is the optimal substructure. Therefore, if F[i][i]=0, we don't need the length1 self-loop for building larger palindromes because any outer edges that would use the self-loop as middle would also produce a shorter even palindrome using the empty middle. Is that always true? Let's verify: Suppose middle is self-loop i->i, outer first edge x->i label a, outer last edge i->y label a. The path labels a c a. The even path x->i->y labels a a is valid and shorter. So yes, the odd path is not the shortest for that pair. But what if the outer first edge and last edge are the same edge? Still valid. So no need.

However, consider a case where the middle self-loop is not at the same vertex as the outer connection? The middle path from u to v of length1 is an edge u->v. If F[u][v] is 0 only when u=v, so a length1 middle cannot be replaced by empty because u≠v. For self-loop u=v, yes replaced by empty. For a non-self-loop direct edge u->v, F[u][v]=1 (no empty), so we need direct edge source. We initialize all direct edges, including self-loops? If we skip self-loop direct edge because dist0, we might miss odd palindromes where middle is self-loop, but those are dominated by even palindromes as argued. But wait, what if the outer edges have labels such that the even palindrome with empty middle is not valid because the middle vertex after stripping outer edges would be i, but the path x->i->y requires edge i->y; that's exactly the last edge. It is valid. So yes dominated.

But let's be absolutely sure: The recurrence F[x][y] = min(2 + F[u][v]) over outer edges. If F[u][v] is the minimum palindrome length from u to v. If u=v, F=0. If there is a self-loop u->u, F=0, so 2+F=2. Using self-loop would give 3, not minimal. So skip self-loop direct edge is fine for shortest answers. But what about the direct edge source P(i,i)=1 being needed as an intermediate state in BFS to generate some P(x,y) for which the even empty-middle path is not valid because the outer edges are not both present? Wait to generate P(x,y) from middle P(i,i) with dist1, reverse BFS uses:
From P(i,i) dist1, right vertex i outgoing to y label a -> M(i,y,a) dist2; incoming to left i label a from x -> P(x,y) dist3.
This requires edge i->y label a and edge x->i label a. If those edges exist, then from P(i,i) dist0, the same reverse transitions would generate P(x,y) dist2 (because dist0 -> M dist1 -> P dist2). So P(x,y) would already have distance2, which is shorter. Thus the dist1 source doesn't improve any P(x,y). Therefore skipping self-loop direct edge is safe for shortest distances. More generally, for any state, we only need the minimum distance; larger distances to the same state cannot lead to a shorter overall distance because all outgoing transitions add the same amount and the graph is deterministic in terms of state. In shortest path problems, keeping only shortest distance to each node is sufficient. Since the expanded graph has a single node P(i,i) representing both empty and self-loop palindromes, the shortest distance to that node is 0. Any path that goes through P(i,i) with distance1 could be replaced by the same path starting with distance0, yielding a shorter total distance to any descendant. Thus we don't need to keep non-minimal distances. This is standard. So skipping self-loop direct edge is correct.

But wait, in the expanded graph, P(i,i) with dist0 and dist1 are the same node. If we only keep dist0, then a path from a source to some target that would use the self-loop as a "terminal" of odd length and then extend outward is represented by going through P(i,i) at dist0 and then extending outward, which yields even length, not odd. But if the target is reached by extending outward from P(i,i), the number of reverse edges from P(i,i) to target is the same regardless of dist. u = pair // N
y = pair % N
This works because pair = u*N+y.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with using `out_edges[v]` for P state where v is right vertex. Suppose P(u,v) has a palindrome path from u to v. To add outer edges, we need an edge from v to y (last edge) and an edge from x to u (first edge). The label of last edge v->y must equal label of first edge x->u. In reverse BFS, from P(u,v), we first choose last edge v->y with label ch, store M(u,y,ch). Then choose first edge x->u with label ch. This is correct.

Now, let's think about if the order of choosing last edge before first edge matters. In forward path, first edge is consumed before last edge, but since they are independent and labels must match, reverse can choose either. Our M state stores left vertex u (after first edge) and right vertex y (before last edge? Actually y is the vertex after last edge, i.e., the final target). Let's define M(u,y,ch) as "we have already matched the left edge into u, and we still need to match the right edge ending at y with label ch." Forward from P(x,y) to M(u,y,ch) consumes first edge x->u. Then from M(u,y,ch) to P(u,v) consumes last edge v->y. So M stores the final target y, not the right vertex before last edge. In reverse from P(u,v), we choose v->y and go to M(u,y,ch). Then from M(u,y,ch), choose x->u and go to P(x,y). Good. So M(u,y,ch) has right vertex y (the new target of the larger palindrome). This is consistent.

Now, let's think about if the BFS over expanded graph with 270k states and 2M transitions is definitely within time. Python 2M iterations is trivial. But wait, the number of M states is N^2*26 = 260k. For each M state popped, we iterate incoming[u][ch]. Sum over all M states = N*E = 1e6. For each P state popped, iterate out_edges[v]. Sum over all P states = N*E = 1e6. So 2M. However, the BFS might pop all M states? Yes, in dense graph, many M states reached. But 260k pops, each with small list. 2M total. Very fast.

Let's verify the sum for M states: M state (u,y,ch) popped if reached. For each such state, iterate incoming[u][ch]. The total over all possible M states (u,y,ch) of len(incoming[u][ch]) = sum_{u,y,ch} indeg_ch(u) = N * sum_{u,ch} indeg_ch(u) = N * E. E<=N^2, so <=1e6. Good. P states: sum_{u,v} outdeg(v) = N * E <=1e6. So total <=2e6. Excellent.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from collections import deque
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    C = [input().strip() for _ in range(N)]

    out_edges = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    incoming = [[[] for _ in range(26)] for _ in range(N)]
    edges = []

    for i in range(N):
        for j, ch in enumerate(C[i]):
            if ch != '-':
                c = ord(ch) - 97
                out_edges[i].append((j, c))
                incoming[j][c].append(i)
                edges.append((i, j))

    pair_count = N * N
    mid_base = pair_count
    total = pair_count + pair_count * 26
    INF = 10 ** 9
    dist = [INF] * total

    q = deque()

    # Empty palindrome: length 0
    for i in range(N):
        idx = i * N + i
        dist[idx] = 0
        q.append(idx)

    # Single-edge palindrome: length 1
    for i, j in edges:
        idx = i * N + j
        if dist[idx] > 1:
            dist[idx] = 1
            q.append(idx)

    while q:
        idx = q.popleft()
        d = dist[idx]

        if idx < pair_count:
            # P(u, v): there is a palindrome path from u to v of length d.
            u = idx // N
            v = idx - u * N
            nd = d + 1

            # Add an outer edge on the right: v -> y with label c.
            # Move to M(u, y, c).
            for y, c in out_edges[v]:
                mid = mid_base + (u * N + y) * 26 + c
                if dist[mid] > nd:
                    dist[mid] = nd
                    q.append(mid)
        else:
            # M(u, y, c): left side is fixed to u, right edge label is c.
            offset = idx - mid_base
            pair = offset // 26
            c = offset - pair * 26
            u = pair // N
            y = pair - u * N
            nd = d + 1

            # Add an outer edge on the left: x -> u with label c.
            # Move to P(x, y).
            for x in incoming[u][c]:
                p = x * N + y
                if dist[p] > nd:
                    dist[p] = nd
                    q.append(p)

    ans = []
    for i in range(N):
        row = []
        for j in range(N):
            d = dist[i * N + j]
            row.append(str(d if d < INF else -1))
        ans.append(" ".join(row))

    print("\n".join(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
